[403] Latin, _Claudus_, lame. This Le Pelletier has before endeavoured, with surprising dexterity, to show to be the Duc de Bordeaux.

[404] This whole line is difficult. _Araigner_, in the Romance language, is said to mean to plead and gain a cause against another. The _court_ and _long_ we had before, in Century VIII., 57, applied to Napoleon, as rising from a soldier to the long-robed Emperor; here he had to refer to Talma to teach him how to carry it. Now, again, it means the rise from a mere dignitary to Kingship.

[405] Latin, _qui_.

[406] _Sparte_ = Paris. The letters _te_ may be considered as one letter, the _e_ being mute. It will then convert, according to the rule of the anagram.

[407] _Phalange_, flag, standard, writes M. le Pelletier. As far as I can discover anything, this statement appears to be entirely erroneous. The word “phalanx,” as connected with matters military, always signifies a body of men in military order. It may in the plural even stand indefinitely for armies. In the singular it is very confusing. It may stand for a party of twenty-eight men or eight thousand (Potter’s “Antiquities,” ii. 56). Danet says six thousand men. Gibbon introduces one of his constantly recurring slovenly phrases about it,—absurd in fact, but philosophic in form. He is comparing the legion with the phalanx (i. 22), and tells us, “It was soon discovered by reflection, as well as by the event,” that it could not contend with the legion. It is obvious here, that if it had been discovered by reflection it would never have been tried by the event. This somewhat justifies the sarcasm of Porson, that schoolboys ought to be set to translate Gibbon into English. As a pleasing variety, Smith sets the number when complete at about sixteen thousand men. One feels inclined to say that these classical guides are conveying us to that ditch, which is never far to seek, when the blind are leading the blind. Martinius, in his curious “Lex. Phil.,” cites Pliny to show that the Africans first fought the Egyptians _fustius_, with staves, which they called _phalanges_ in the plural, or _phalanx_ in the singular, so that it is an African word. ‎‏פלג‏‎, in Hebrew _phalag_, is a staff. Also there is a spider called _phalangion_; and as the pikes of the first five ranks in a _phalanx_ were interlaced, they were somewhat like a web of staves. Many may regard all this as extremely useless; perhaps they will concede that it is curious; if so, I shall consider it to be useful. I have long found that whatever is very curious is useful. It sets the mind agreeably in movement, and often throws a new light on things that previously had no interest and seemed obscure. At any rate, we have now established that _phalange_ is not to be rendered _standard_, but troops, army.

[408] Latin, _supremus_, the last.

[409] The order is, “Quand l’Empire sera exanclé par trois subrogés.”

[410] Latin, _subrogatus_, substituted; read _subrogés_.

[411] _Exancillatus_, subdued to, under the yoke of.

[412] Latin, _charta_, paper.

[413] Romance, _ne leu_, not read.

[414] _Sainct Memire_ is the anagram of S’Meri. To make this pass, M. le Pelletier drops one _m_ and one _e_.

[415] _Tante_ is to be read _tente_.

[416] _Leans_ is an old word for _là dedans_.

[417] Latin, _perplexus_, troubled.

[418] _Rebors_ = _rebours_; _à rebours_ is against the grain.

[419] _Ognion_ is _Ogmius_, the Celtic Mercury or Gallic Hercules. It has figured in 1792, 1848, and again after the German war, 1872. At the two first periods they put the figure of this Hercules on their five franc pieces, with that idle exergue, _Liberté, Égalité, Fraternité_. This vocable, _Ogmion_, is equivalent to _Oignon_, onion, or bulbous root of the lily. If there could be any doubt at all about this, Nostradamus has taken care to remove it, calling it _le grand mercure d’Hercule fleur de lys_, in Century X. 79.

[420] _Tiers_, pour _le tiers_ État. The third order now claiming to govern itself.

[421] _De bas haut_, for _de bas en haut_ elevated from a low to a higher and conspicuous position.

[422] Latin, _qui_, he, who.

[423] The third estate will serve, as a toe of the foot to the first rank, a stepping-stone to place.

[424] Lucca and Pisa stand for all Tuscany, where, in 1831, Louis Napoleon and Charles Bonaparte, his elder brother, had, with one cannon served by himself and a few Italian partisans, possessed himself of _Civita-Castellana_, in the Pontifical States.

[425] Latin, _decidium_, a word not of classical usage, signifying ruin, or a fall.

[426] Romance _sang_, family.

[427] Latin, _regnum_, empire.

[428] _Lectoyre_, a town in France, in the department of Gers. Garencières adopts the variant Lectoure, which he says is a town in Gascony. The word occurs again, Century VII. 12, and there he says it is a city of Guyenne. _Lectora_ was the Latin name. It was a place of great strength, and picturesque; but, unless we can find it to be an anagram, there is nothing to connect the town in any way with the fortunes of Louis Napoleon.

[429] The word _pleira_, is of course, only an archaic transposition of _pliera_. The line here can in no way be made to scan, being two syllables or one whole foot short. I would suggest it be read Nepveu (du sang) par peur pleira l’enseigne.

[430] _Ogmion_ has been explained already to be the symbol of the French Republic.

[431] The _texte-type_ reads _et amy au_.

[432] _My-hom_, Le Pelletier says, is _demy-homme_, a man of low birth, and Roy _de longue_ = _de vielle race_. I prefer to read _de longue vie_, and understand the King of Prussia; and _my-hom_ I take to be the bivalve-man, or Bismarck.

[433] Latin, _classis_, band; à classe, by a band [sic?]. It might be better here to render _classe_ as “crash,” κλάσις, _brisure_. _De nuict la classe_, a crash by night.

[434] Latin, _arca_, chest, or dam.

[435] Latin, _demolitus_, demolished.

[436] Latin, _antecaptus_, seized beforehand.

[437] Latin, _dux_, leader.

[438] Latin, _unus_, the first.

[439] Latin, _cavea_, theatre.

[440] Romance, _incogneus_, unknown.

[441] The four were Orsini, Pieri, de Rudio, and Gomez.

[442] Greek, Δαν-ἀρχή, as we had it before (_v._ p. 289).

[443] _Frise_, Hanover.

[444] Latin, _dependere_, to weigh out, spend.

[445] M. le Pelletier calculates that the golden mark was equal to 250 grammes, or 100,000 marks = 10,000,000 francs.

[446] Latin, _oratio_, harangue.

[447] Romance, _prins_, _pris_, taken.

[448] Latin, _decisus_, cut off.

[449] _Delà_, for _au delà_.

[450] Italian, _vappa_, scamp.

[451] Latin, _subito_, suddenly.

[452] _Il tournera_, for _il fera tourner_.

[453] Latin, _propinque_, at hand, near.

[454] “Stands some five feet ten or more; a man of strong solid stature, and dignified, now partly military carriage: the expression of him valour and devout intelligence,—energy and delicacy on a basis of simplicity. Fifty-four years old, gone April last; ruddy-fair complexion, bronzed by toil and age; light brown hair and moustache are getting streaked with grey. A figure of sufficient impressiveness;—not lovely to the man-milliner species, nor pretending to be so. Massive stature; big massive head, of somewhat leonine aspect, ‘evident workshop and storehouse of a vast treasury of natural parts.’ Wart above the right eyebrow; nose of considerable blunt aquiline proportions; strict yet copious lips, full of all tremulous sensibilities, and also, if need were, of all fierceness and rigours; deep loving eyes, call them grave, call them stern, looking from under those craggy brows, as if in life-long sorrow, and yet not thinking it sorrow, thinking it only labour, and endeavour:—on the whole, a right noble lion-face, and hero-face.” (Carlyle, “Cromwell Letters,” ii. p. 435).
